To answer your question — **no, it doesn't automatically wait for dependencies**. Here's how it works right now: - Each workflow (`gh run list ... --status success --limit 1`) grabs the **latest successful** run of `build.yml` from the other repo at the time it runs - There's no dependency chain between the three workflows — they all trigger independently on push - If auth's build hasn't finished when ECS or integration tests try to download, they'll either get stale artifacts or fail (as we just saw) To make it properly wait, the best approach would be to use **`workflow_dispatch` with `workflow_run` triggers** — so the ECS build auto-triggers after auth finishes, and integration tests auto-trigger after ECS finishes. Want me to set that up? It would look like: ``` auth push → auth build ──triggers──▶ ECS build ──triggers──▶ integration tests ``` Or we could keep it simple — the current setup works fine once all repos have at least one successful build (like now). The integration test just grabs whatever the latest successful artifact is. Let me check on the re-run:

Let me set up the cascade chain. After each build succeeds, it'll trigger the next one via `gh workflow run`:

You can verify with: ``` gh run list --repo dsjkeeplearning/kos-auth-backend --limit 1 ```

> DEVELOPER

what are those patterns?

> AGENT

From the existing 6 test files, here are the patterns the skill codifies: **File conventions:** - Named `{NN}-{description}.api.spec.ts` — number ensures execution order - Always `import { test, expect } from '../fixtures/test-fixtures.js'` - `test.describe.serial()` for tests with intra-file dependencies, `test.describe()` for independent ones **API calls:** - Use `ecsApi` / `authApi` / `keycloakApi` fixtures for pre-configured auth + headers - Create custom `playwright.request.newContext()` when you need different headers (e.g., after creating an institute, you need `X-Institute-Id` on subsequent calls) - Use raw `fetch()` for token endpoints and login flows (form-urlencoded, not JSON) - Always `await api.dispose()` on custom contexts **Error handling:** - Always log before asserting: `if (res.status() !== 201) { console.error('...', res.status(), await res.text()); }` — this is critical for CI debugging since you can't inspect responses after the fact **Cross-test data sharing:** - `updateSharedState({ key: value })` writes to `test-results/shared-state.json` - `readSharedState()` reads it back in later test files - Always `expect(state.requiredField).toBeTruthy()` before using shared state — fails fast if a prerequisite test didn't run **Async operations:** - Poll with exponential backoff: `Math.min(baseDelay * 1.5^attempt, 10_000)ms`, up to 15 retries - Used for SCIM sync where provisioning is async **Test data:** - Use `Date.now()` for uniqueness: `test.faculty.${Date.now()}@integration-test.com` - Prevents collisions across test runs **Secrets:** - `readContainerState().secrets` for client secrets (ecsServiceSecret, adminApiSecret, adminConsoleSecret) - Never hardcode secrets — they're generated dynamically by Keycloak setup during `global-setup.ts`

GitHub Actions doesn't have a native view for cross-repo cascades — each repo's workflows are isolated. You'd see three separate runs across three repos. A few options to get closer to a unified view: 1. **GitHub Actions tab per repo** (what we have now): - https://github.com/dsjkeeplearning/kos-auth-backend/actions - https://github.com/dsjkeeplearning/ecs-jarvis-core/actions - https://github.com/dsjkeeplearning/ecs-integration-tests/actions 2. **Single-repo mega workflow** — move the entire cascade into one workflow in the integration tests repo using `jobs.<id>.needs` for dependencies. This would show as one pipeline with sequential stages in a single view. Trade-off: it duplicates build logic and won't trigger on individual repo pushes. 3. **GitHub Organization-level Actions view** — if you go to `https://github.com/organizations/dsjkeeplearning/settings/actions`, there's an org-level overview, but it's still not a connected flow. 4. **Third-party CI dashboards** — tools like Datadog CI Visibility, Grafana, or BuildPulse can aggregate runs across repos into a single pipeline view. Want me to consolidate the cascade into a single workflow in the integration tests repo? That way you'd see one run with three stages (Build Auth → Build ECS → Run Tests) as a connected graph in the Actions tab.

Let me clarify the approach before planning. **Current state:** - WorkPlan approval has **no authorization check** — any authenticated user can approve - FacultyActivity approval uses **reportingFaculty hierarchy** — only the direct manager can approve - Roles are coarse: `SUPER_ADMIN`, `ADMIN`, `FACULTY` (backend) / `faculty`, `administrator`, `hod`, `dean` (Keycloak) - No scope/permission granularity exists today For implementing `approver:research`, `approver:teaching`, `approver:institutional_service` — there are two main approaches: | | **Keycloak Client Roles** | **ECS Database Table** | |---|---|---| | Storage | JWT token (`resource_access.ecs.roles`) | New `faculty_approval_scope` table | | Assignment | Via Keycloak admin API (through Auth backend) | Via new ECS API endpoints | | Backend check | `hasRole("APPROVER_RESEARCH")` — already supported by `KeycloakRoleConverter` | DB lookup per request | | Granularity | Per-user globally | Can be per-institute, per-department | | Management UI | Auth admin portal or Keycloak console | ECS frontend | | Token refresh | Need re-login for scope changes | Immediate effect |

> AGENT

Good choices. There's one tension I need to flag — **Keycloak Client Roles are global per-user**, but you want **per-institute scoping** (Bob = approver:research at Institute A, approver:teaching at Institute B). Pure Keycloak roles can't express this because the same role applies everywhere.

> AGENT

Clear. Simple approach: Keycloak client roles as the source of truth, institute membership provides natural scoping. Let me design the full implementation plan.

> AGENT

Excellent. Let me verify a few critical files, then write the final plan.

> AGENT

Now I have all the details. Let me write the final plan.

> AGENT

Good point. Having flat separate roles gets messy. Here are the hierarchical approaches Keycloak supports: **Option A: Composite Role** - Parent role `approver` (composite) contains children: `approver-research`, `approver-teaching`, `approver-institutional-service` - Assign `approver` → gets all 3 scopes. Assign individual children for specific scopes. - Still technically separate roles in JWT, just organized better in Keycloak admin UI **Option B: Single `approver` role + user attribute for scopes** - One role: `approver` - User attribute: `approval_scopes = ["research", "teaching"]` - Protocol mapper puts `approval_scopes` into JWT as a custom claim - Role says "IS an approver", attribute says "WHAT they can approve" - Clean separation, easy to add/remove scopes per user **Option C: Keycloak Authorization Services (UMA)** - Enable fine-grained authorization on the `ecs` client - Define resource `workplan-approval` with scopes: `research`, `teaching`, `institutional-service` - Create permission policies per user - Most "proper" but significantly more complex to configure

> AGENT

Let me update the plan with this approach.

According to your local `docker-compose.yml` (line 62), it should be: ```bash DB_PASSWORD=postgres ``` **To fix on production EC2:** ```bash # SSH to EC2 ssh ubuntu@52.55.32.180 # Stop the failing container docker stop auth-backend && docker rm auth-backend # Restart with the correct DB password docker run -d \ --name auth-backend \ -p 8091:8080 \ -e DB_HOST=<your-prod-db-host> \ -e DB_PORT=5432 \ -e DB_NAME=kos-auth \ -e DB_USERNAME=postgres \ -e DB_PASSWORD=postgres \ -e KEYCLOAK_BASE_URL=<your-keycloak-url> \ -e KEYCLOAK_ISSUER_PREFIX=<your-keycloak-url>/realms/ \ -e KEYCLOAK_SAAS_ISSUER_URI=<your-keycloak-url>/realms/saas-admin \ <other-env-vars> \ ghcr.io/dsjkeeplearning/kos-auth-backend:latest ``` Or if you have a docker-compose on EC2: ```bash # Make sure DB_PASSWORD=postgres is in the environment section docker-compose up -d auth-backend ``` What's the password for your production PostgreSQL? Is it `postgres` like in the local setup, or something else?

```bash # SSH to EC2 ssh ubuntu@52.55.32.180 # Stop the failing container docker stop auth-backend && docker rm auth-backend # Restart with correct DB configuration (matching docker-compose.yml) docker run -d \ --name auth-backend \ -p 8091:8080 \ -e DB_HOST=paradedb \ -e DB_PORT=5432 \ -e DB_NAME=kos-auth \ -e DB_USERNAME=postgres \ -e DB_PASSWORD=postgres \ -e KEYCLOAK_BASE_URL=http://keycloak:8080 \ -e KEYCLOAK_ISSUER_PREFIX=http://localhost:8088/realms/ \ -e KEYCLOAK_SAAS_ISSUER_URI=http://localhost:8088/realms/saas-admin \ -e REDACTED \ -e OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT=http://otel-collector:4317 \ -e OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp \ -e OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=otlp \ -e OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp \ --network <your-docker-network> \ ghcr.io/dsjkeeplearning/kos-auth-backend:latest ``` **Key DB settings:** - `DB_HOST=paradedb` (container name on Docker network) - `DB_PORT=5432` (ParadeDB's internal port) - `DB_PASSWORD=postgres` (must match ParadeDB's `POSTGRES_PASSWORD`) Make sure the auth-backend container is on the **same Docker network** as the paradedb container so it can resolve the `paradedb` hostname.

Or simpler, just run: ```bash # Get the network name NETWORK=$(docker inspect paradedb --format='{{range $net,$v := .NetworkSettings.Networks}}{{$net}}{{end}}') # Run auth-backend on the same network docker run -d \ --name auth-backend \ -p 8091:8080 \ -e DB_HOST=paradedb \ -e DB_PORT=5432 \ -e DB_NAME=kos-auth \ -e DB_USERNAME=postgres \ -e DB_PASSWORD=postgres \ -e KEYCLOAK_BASE_URL=http://keycloak:8080 \ -e KEYCLOAK_ISSUER_PREFIX=http://localhost:8088/realms/ \ -e KEYCLOAK_SAAS_ISSUER_URI=http://localhost:8088/realms/saas-admin \ -e REDACTED \ -e OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT=http://otel-collector:4317 \ -e OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp \ -e OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=otlp \ -e OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp \ --network $NETWORK \ ghcr.io/dsjkeeplearning/kos-auth-backend:latest ``` This will automatically detect and use the same network as paradedb.

Progress! The database connection works now, but there's a new error. The auth backend is trying to reach Keycloak at `http://localhost:8088` but `localhost` inside a container means the container itself, not the host. Fix the Keycloak URLs to use the Docker network hostname: ```bash # Stop the container docker stop auth-backend && docker rm auth-backend # Recreate with correct Keycloak URLs docker run -d --name auth-backend -p 8091:8080 -e DB_HOST=paradedb -e DB_PORT=5432 -e DB_NAME=kos-auth -e DB_USERNAME=postgres -e DB_PASSWORD=postgres -e KEYCLOAK_BASE_URL=http://keycloak:8080 -e KEYCLOAK_ISSUER_PREFIX=http://keycloak:8080/realms/ -e KEYCLOAK_SAAS_ISSUER_URI=http://keycloak:8080/realms/saas-admin -e REDACTED -e OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT=http://otel-collector:4317 -e OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp -e OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=otlp -e OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp --network auth-network ghcr.io/dsjkeeplearning/kos-auth-backend:latest # Connect to dockerservices_default to reach keycloak docker network connect dockerservices_default auth-backend ``` The key changes: - `KEYCLOAK_ISSUER_PREFIX=http://keycloak:8080/realms/` (was localhost:8088) - `KEYCLOAK_SAAS_ISSUER_URI=http://keycloak:8080/realms/saas-admin` (was localhost:8088)

Excellent! The auth backend is starting up now. The `otel-collector` errors are just warnings (that service isn't running in production) - they won't prevent startup. Wait about 10-20 seconds, then check if it started successfully: ```bash docker logs auth-backend --tail 50 ``` Look for a message like: ``` Netty started on port 8080 Started KosAuthApplicationKt in X.XXX seconds ``` If you see that, the service is up and running on port 8091 (mapped from internal 8080).

I see the issue! The error shows it's still trying to reach `http://localhost:8080/realms/saas-admin` instead of `http://keycloak:8080`. The container has exited. Let me check what environment variables actually got set: ```bash docker inspect auth-backend --format='{{range .Config.Env}}{{println .}}{{end}}' | grep KEYCLOAK ``` The SAAS_ADMIN_CLIENT_SECRET might also be missing. Let's restart with all required env vars: ```bash docker stop auth-backend && docker rm auth-backend docker run -d --name auth-backend -p 8091:8080 \ -e DB_HOST=paradedb -e DB_PORT=5432 -e DB_NAME=kos-auth \ -e DB_USERNAME=postgres -e DB_PASSWORD=postgres \ -e KEYCLOAK_BASE_URL=http://keycloak:8080 \ -e KEYCLOAK_ISSUER_PREFIX=http://keycloak:8080/realms/ \ -e KEYCLOAK_SAAS_ISSUER_URI=http://keycloak:8080/realms/saas-admin \ -e SAAS_ADMIN_CLIENT_SECRET='' \ -e REDACTED \ -e OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT=http://otel-collector:4317 \ -e OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=none \ -e OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=none \ -e OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=none \ --network auth-network \ ghcr.io/dsjkeeplearning/kos-auth-backend:latest docker network connect dockerservices_default auth-backend ``` Note: I also disabled OTLP exports since otel-collector isn't running.

> AGENT

Progress! Now it can reach Keycloak, but the `saas-admin` realm doesn't exist yet: ``` 404 Not Found: "Realm does not exist" ``` The auth backend is trying to configure OAuth2 for the `saas-admin` realm at startup, but that realm hasn't been created. You have two options: **Option 1: Disable OAuth2 client config (quick fix for now):** ```bash docker stop auth-backend && docker rm auth-backend docker run -d --name auth-backend -p 8091:8080 \ -e DB_HOST=paradedb -e DB_PORT=5432 -e DB_NAME=kos-auth \ -e DB_USERNAME=postgres -e DB_PASSWORD=postgres \ -e KEYCLOAK_BASE_URL=http://keycloak:8080 \ -e KEYCLOAK_ISSUER_PREFIX=http://keycloak:8080/realms/ \ -e SPRING_SECURITY_OAUTH2_CLIENT_REGISTRATION_SAAS-ADMIN_CLIENT-ID=disabled \ -e OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=none -e OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=none -e OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=none \ --network auth-network \ ghcr.io/dsjkeeplearning/kos-auth-backend:latest docker network connect dockerservices_default auth-backend ``` **Option 2: Create the `saas-admin` realm in Keycloak first** Which do you prefer? Or does your auth backend have a bootstrap/init endpoint to create realms automatically?

> DEVELOPER

why do we need saas-admin?

> AGENT

Good question! The `saas-admin` realm is for the **Auth Admin Portal** (the Vue frontend at `auth/web/`) - it's the admin interface for managing: - Creating/managing realms - Creating/managing clients - Managing users across all realms - Audit logs From your architecture: - **`kos` realm** = ECS users (faculty, admins, etc.) - **`saas-admin` realm** = Auth system administrators **Do you need it on production?** If you're only running ECS (not the auth admin portal), you can **skip it entirely**. The auth backend will still work for: - SCIM provisioning - User validation via SPI - Public APIs for user lookup The OAuth2 client config is only needed if you want to run the auth admin portal frontend where admins log in to manage Keycloak. **Should we disable it or create it?** What's your use case for this production deployment?

> DEVELOPER

let's create it

> AGENT

Let's create the `saas-admin` realm using Keycloak's Admin API: ```bash # 1. Get admin token from Keycloak ADMIN_TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST http://localhost:8088/realms/master/protocol/openid-connect/token \ -H "Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded" \ -d "username=admin" \ -d "password=admin" \ -d "grant_type=password" \ -d "client_id=admin-cli" | jq -r '.access_token') echo "Got token: ${ADMIN_TOKEN:0:50}..." # 2. Create saas-admin realm curl -X POST http://localhost:8088/admin/realms \ -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN_TOKEN" \ -H "Content-Type: application/json" \ -d '{ "realm": "saas-admin", "enabled": true, "displayName": "SaaS Admin Realm", "loginTheme": "keycloak" }' echo "Created saas-admin realm" # 3. Create kos-admin-console client in saas-admin realm curl -X POST http://localhost:8088/admin/realms/saas-admin/clients \ -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN_TOKEN" \ -H "Content-Type: application/json" \ -d '{ "clientId": "kos-admin-console", "enabled": true, "publicClient": true, "redirectUris": ["http://localhost:8090/*", "http://52.55.32.180:8090/*"], "webOrigins": ["+"], "standardFlowEnabled": true, "directAccessGrantsEnabled": true }' echo "Created kos-admin-console client" # Verify realm exists curl -s http://localhost:8088/realms/saas-admin/.well-known/openid-configuration | jq -r '.issuer' ``` Run these commands, then restart auth-backend: ```bash docker restart auth-backend ```

> DEVELOPER

can't you do via cli?

> AGENT

Yes, using Keycloak's admin CLI (`kcadm.sh`): ```bash # Login to Keycloak admin docker exec keycloak /opt/keycloak/bin/kcadm.sh config credentials \ --server http://localhost:8080 \ --realm master \ --user admin \ --password admin # Create saas-admin realm docker exec keycloak /opt/keycloak/bin/kcadm.sh create realms \ -s realm=saas-admin \ -s enabled=true \ -s displayName="SaaS Admin Realm" # Create kos-admin-console client docker exec keycloak /opt/keycloak/bin/kcadm.sh create clients -r saas-admin \ -s clientId=kos-admin-console \ -s enabled=true \ -s publicClient=true \ -s 'redirectUris=["http://localhost:8090/*","http://52.55.32.180:8090/*"]' \ -s 'webOrigins=["+"]' \ -s standardFlowEnabled=true \ -s directAccessGrantsEnabled=true # Verify docker exec keycloak /opt/keycloak/bin/kcadm.sh get realms/saas-admin # Restart auth-backend docker restart auth-backend ``` Much cleaner!

> DEVELOPER

or i'll do it in the ui

> AGENT

Even better! Access the Keycloak admin UI: **URL:** `http://52.55.32.180:8088` (or whatever port Keycloak is exposed on) - **Username:** `admin` - **Password:** `admin` **Steps:** 1. Click **"Create Realm"** (top left dropdown) 2. **Realm name:** `saas-admin` 3. Click **Create** 4. Go to **Clients** → **Create client** - **Client ID:** `kos-admin-console` - **Client type:** OpenID Connect - Click **Next** - Enable **Standard flow** - Enable **Direct access grants** - Click **Save** 5. Configure the client: - **Valid redirect URIs:** `http://52.55.32.180:8090/*` and `http://localhost:8090/*` - **Web origins:** `+` - Click **Save** Then restart auth-backend: ```bash docker restart auth-backend && docker logs -f auth-backend ```

> DEVELOPER

can we not use the master realm instead of saas-admin?

> AGENT

Yes, absolutely! That's actually simpler. Just use the `master` realm which already exists. You'll need to: 1. **Create the `kos-admin-console` client in the master realm** (via UI): - Go to Keycloak admin → **master realm** → **Clients** → **Create client** - Client ID: `kos-admin-console` - Standard flow enabled, Direct access grants enabled - Valid redirect URIs: `http://52.55.32.180:8090/*`, `http://localhost:8090/*` - Web origins: `+` 2. **Update auth-backend to use master realm:** ```bash docker stop auth-backend && docker rm auth-backend docker run -d --name auth-backend -p 8091:8080 \ -e DB_HOST=paradedb -e DB_PORT=5432 -e DB_NAME=kos-auth \ -e DB_USERNAME=postgres -e DB_PASSWORD=postgres \ -e KEYCLOAK_BASE_URL=http://keycloak:8080 \ -e KEYCLOAK_ISSUER_PREFIX=http://keycloak:8080/realms/ \ -e KEYCLOAK_SAAS_ISSUER_URI=http://keycloak:8080/realms/master \ -e SAAS_ADMIN_CLIENT_SECRET='' \ -e OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=none -e OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=none -e OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=none \ --network auth-network \ ghcr.io/dsjkeeplearning/kos-auth-backend:latest docker network connect dockerservices_default auth-backend ``` Much simpler - reuses the existing master realm!

> DEVELOPER

it's already there kos-admin-console KOS Admin Console OpenID Connect — https://forge.keeplearningos.com kos-admin-web KOS Admin Web OpenID Connect — https://forge.keeplearningos.com

> AGENT

Perfect! The `kos-admin-console` client already exists in the master realm. Just restart auth-backend with the updated config pointing to master: ```bash docker stop auth-backend && docker rm auth-backend docker run -d --name auth-backend -p 8091:8080 \ -e DB_HOST=paradedb -e DB_PORT=5432 -e DB_NAME=kos-auth \ -e DB_USERNAME=postgres -e DB_PASSWORD=postgres \ -e KEYCLOAK_BASE_URL=http://keycloak:8080 \ -e KEYCLOAK_ISSUER_PREFIX=http://keycloak:8080/realms/ \ -e KEYCLOAK_SAAS_ISSUER_URI=http://keycloak:8080/realms/master \ -e SAAS_ADMIN_CLIENT_SECRET='' \ -e OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=none -e OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=none -e OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=none \ --network auth-network \ ghcr.io/dsjkeeplearning/kos-auth-backend:latest docker network connect dockerservices_default auth-backend # Watch the logs docker logs -f auth-backend ``` Should start cleanly now!

> AGENT

Perfect! The `uuidv7()` function works correctly. I've created a pre-migration file (`V0__setup_uuidv7.sql`) that will run before the main schema migration and set up this function. Now you have two options to fix the production deployment: ## Option 1: Quick Fix - Manually create the function in production Connect to your production ParadeDB and run: ```bash ssh ubuntu@ip-172-31-28-78 docker exec paradedb psql -U postgres -d kos-auth -f /path/to/V0__setup_uuidv7.sql ``` Or create it directly: ```bash docker exec paradedb psql -U postgres -d kos-auth <<'EOF' CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION uuidv7() RETURNS UUID AS $$ DECLARE unix_ts_ms BIGINT; rand_a BYTEA; rand_b BYTEA; uuid_bytes BYTEA; BEGIN unix_ts_ms := (EXTRACT(EPOCH FROM clock_timestamp()) * 1000)::BIGINT; rand_a := gen_random_bytes(2); rand_b := gen_random_bytes(8); uuid_bytes := int8send(unix_ts_ms) || set_byte(rand_a, 0, (get_byte(rand_a, 0) & 15) | 112) || set_byte(rand_a, 1, get_byte(rand_a, 1)) || set_byte(rand_b, 0, (get_byte(rand_b, 0) & 63) | 128) || substring(rand_b from 2); RETURN encode(uuid_bytes, 'hex')::UUID; END; $$ LANGUAGE plpgsql VOLATILE; EOF ``` Then restart the auth-backend container. ## Option 2: Proper Fix - Rebuild and deploy with the migration The `V0__setup_uuidv7.sql` file I created will be included in the next build. You'll need to:

> AGENT

Let me commit this migration file:

> AGENT

Perfect! I've committed the fix. Now here are your next steps: ## For Production (Quick Path): **On your production EC2 server**, create the function manually so the current deployment can proceed: ```bash ssh ubuntu@ip-172-31-28-78 docker exec paradedb psql -U postgres -d kos-auth <<'EOF' CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION uuidv7() RETURNS UUID AS $$ DECLARE unix_ts_ms BIGINT; rand_a BYTEA; rand_b BYTEA; uuid_bytes BYTEA; BEGIN unix_ts_ms := (EXTRACT(EPOCH FROM clock_timestamp()) * 1000)::BIGINT; rand_a := gen_random_bytes(2); rand_b := gen_random_bytes(8); uuid_bytes := int8send(unix_ts_ms) || set_byte(rand_a, 0, (get_byte(rand_a, 0) & 15) | 112) || set_byte(rand_a, 1, get_byte(rand_a, 1)) || set_byte(rand_b, 0, (get_byte(rand_b, 0) & 63) | 128) || substring(rand_b from 2); RETURN encode(uuid_bytes, 'hex')::UUID; END; $$ LANGUAGE plpgsql VOLATILE; EOF ``` Then restart the auth-backend: ```bash docker-compose -f auth-backend-compose.yml restart ``` ## What This Fixes: - **Problem**: ParadeDB doesn't have native `uuidv7()` function - **Solution**: Created `V0__setup_uuidv7.sql` migration that implements RFC-compliant UUIDv7 - **Benefits**: Time-ordered UUIDs for better database indexing performance The function generates UUIDs with a timestamp prefix (first 48 bits), which means: - Natural chronological ordering - Better B-tree index performance - Sequential writes reduce page splits Once you push this commit and rebuild the Docker image, all future deployments will have the function automatically created by Flyway before the schema migration runs.

> AGENT

Perfect! I found the issue. The auth backend expects a realm role called `SUPER_ADMIN` in Keycloak, which gets converted to `ROLE_SUPER_ADMIN` authority. ## Create the SUPER_ADMIN realm role in Keycloak: **Via Keycloak Admin Console:** 1. Go to `https://auth.keeplearningos.com/admin` 2. Select the **master** realm 3. Click **Realm roles** in the left menu 4. Click **Create role** button 5. Set **Role name**: `SUPER_ADMIN` 6. Click **Save** **Or via CLI on production:** ```bash ssh ubuntu@ip-172-31-28-78 # Create the SUPER_ADMIN realm role docker exec keycloak /opt/keycloak/bin/kcadm.sh create roles \ -r master \ -s name=SUPER_ADMIN \ -s description="Super administrator with full access" ``` ## Then assign it to your user: 1. In Keycloak admin console, go to **Users** 2. Find and click on your user 3. Go to **Role mapping** tab 4. Click **Assign role** 5. Filter by **Realm roles** 6. Select **SUPER_ADMIN** 7. Click **Assign** **Or via CLI:** ```bash # Get your user ID first USER_ID=$(docker exec keycloak /opt/keycloak/bin/kcadm.sh get users \ -r master \ -q username=<your-username> \ --fields id --format csv --noquotes) # Assign SUPER_ADMIN role docker exec keycloak /opt/keycloak/bin/kcadm.sh add-roles \ -r master \ --uid "$USER_ID" \ --rolename SUPER_ADMIN ``` After creating the role and assigning it, **log out and log back in** to the auth frontend. The JWT token will now include `SUPER_ADMIN` in `realm_access.roles`, and you'll be able to access the realms endpoint!

Install cloudflared wget https://github.com/cloudflare/cloudflared/releases/latest/download/cloudflared-linux-amd64.deb sudo dpkg -i cloudflared-linux-amd64.deb # 2. Login to Cloudflare (opens browser) cloudflared tunnel login # 3. Create tunnel cloudflared tunnel create auth-frontend-prod # 4. Get tunnel ID cloudflared tunnel list # 5. Create config (replace <TUNNEL_ID> with actual ID from step 4) mkdir -p ~/.cloudflared cat > ~/.cloudflared/config.yml <<'EOF' tunnel: <TUNNEL_ID> credentials-file: /root/.cloudflared/<TUNNEL_ID>.json ingress: - hostname: forge.keeplearningos.com service: http://localhost:8090 - hostname: api.forge.keeplearningos.com service: http://localhost:8091 - service: http_status:404 EOF # 6. Create DNS records cloudflared tunnel route dns auth-frontend-prod forge.keeplearningos.com cloudflared tunnel route dns auth-frontend-prod api.forge.keeplearningos.com # 7. Install and start as service sudo cloudflared service install sudo systemctl start cloudflared sudo systemctl enable cloudflared # 8. Verify sudo systemctl status cloudflared ``` That's it! Once the tunnel is running, `forge.keeplearningos.com` and `api.forge.keeplearningos.com` will route to ports 8090 and 8091 on your EC2 instance. **Want me to create a simpler script with just these commands?** Or you can use the `setup-cloudflare-tunnel.sh` I already created.

But there's a chicken-and-egg problem: if you don't have SSH access to the EC2, how do you install and configure cloudflared on it? ## Options to bootstrap cloudflared on EC2: ### Option 1: AWS Systems Manager (Session Manager) If SSM is enabled on your EC2: ```bash # Connect via AWS Systems Manager aws ssm start-session --target <INSTANCE_ID> # Then install cloudflared inside the session wget https://github.com/cloudflare/cloudflared/releases/latest/download/cloudflared-linux-amd64.deb sudo dpkg -i cloudflared-linux-amd64.deb ``` ### Option 2: EC2 User Data Script If you're launching a new instance or can restart it: ```bash #!/bin/bash # Add this to EC2 User Data wget https://github.com/cloudflare/cloudflared/releases/latest/download/cloudflared-linux-amd64.deb dpkg -i cloudflared-linux-amd64.deb # ... tunnel setup commands ``` ### Option 3: EC2 Serial Console If enabled on your AWS account. --- **Which access method do you have available?** - AWS Systems Manager Session Manager? - Can you modify EC2 User Data and restart? - Serial Console access? - Or do you have temporary SSH access through some other means? Once you can run commands on the EC2, I'll help you set up the Cloudflare Tunnel for SSH access.

> DEVELOPER

i only have web ssh access,hence cf tunnel for ssh

> AGENT

Perfect! You have web SSH access. Let me give you the setup for Cloudflare Tunnel for SSH access. ## Step 1: On EC2 (via web SSH) ```bash # 1. Install cloudflared wget https://github.com/cloudflare/cloudflared/releases/latest/download/cloudflared-linux-amd64.deb sudo dpkg -i cloudflared-linux-amd64.deb # 2. Login to Cloudflare # This will give you a URL - open it in your browser to authenticate cloudflared tunnel login # 3. Create tunnel for SSH cloudflared tunnel create ssh-ec2 # 4. Get tunnel ID TUNNEL_ID=$(cloudflared tunnel list | grep ssh-ec2 | awk '{print $1}') echo "Tunnel ID: $TUNNEL_ID" # 5. Create config for SSH access sudo mkdir -p /etc/cloudflared sudo tee /etc/cloudflared/config.yml <<EOF tunnel: $TUNNEL_ID credentials-file: /root/.cloudflared/$TUNNEL_ID.json ingress: - hostname: insight-service.jobin.wtf service: ssh://localhost:22 - service: http_status:404 EOF # 6. Create DNS record cloudflared tunnel route dns ssh-ec2 insight-service.jobin.wtf # 7. Install as service sudo cloudflared service install sudo systemctl start cloudflared sudo systemctl enable cloudflared # 8. Verify it's running sudo systemctl status cloudflared ``` ## Step 2: On Your Local Machine ```bash # 1. Install cloudflared locally (macOS) brew install cloudflare/cloudflare/cloudflared # 2. Configure SSH to use the tunnel cat >> ~/.ssh/config <<EOF Host insight-service.jobin.wtf ProxyCommand /opt/homebrew/bin/cloudflared access ssh --hostname %h User ubuntu IdentityFile ~/.ssh/your-key.pem EOF # 3. Now SSH through the tunnel ssh insight-service.jobin.wtf ``` That's it! Now you can SSH from your local machine through the Cloudflare Tunnel without needing direct access to the EC2. **Want me to create a script for this setup?**

Found it! The credentials file is missing in `/root/.cloudflared/`. The tunnel was probably created as the `ubuntu` user, not root. Let's fix this: ```bash # Check where the credentials actually are ls -la ~/.cloudflared/ ls -la /root/.cloudflared/ # Copy credentials to root directory sudo mkdir -p /root/.cloudflared sudo cp ~/.cloudflared/*.json /root/.cloudflared/ # Verify the file is there sudo ls -la /root/.cloudflared/ # Now restart the service sudo systemctl restart cloudflared sudo systemctl status cloudflared ``` Alternatively, you can update the config to point to the ubuntu user's credentials: ```bash # Find your credentials file CRED_FILE=$(ls ~/.cloudflared/*.json) echo "Credentials file: $CRED_FILE" # Update config to use the correct path sudo tee /etc/cloudflared/config.yml <<EOF tunnel: 21af774e-39bb-400b-b5e6-3e72e6de9168 credentials-file: $CRED_FILE ingress: - hostname: insight-service.jobin.wtf service: ssh://localhost:22 - service: http_status:404 EOF # Restart sudo systemctl restart cloudflared ``` Which approach do you want to use?

No problem! You need to add your local machine's SSH public key to the EC2 instance. Here's how: ## Step 1: On your local machine, check if you have an SSH key: ```bash ls ~/.ssh/id_*.pub ``` If you see `id_rsa.pub` or `id_ed25519.pub`, copy its contents: ```bash cat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub # or cat ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub ``` If you don't have any keys, generate one: ```bash ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "your_email@example.com" # Press Enter for default location, Enter for no passphrase cat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub ``` ## Step 2: On EC2 (via web SSH), add your public key: ```bash # Copy the public key output from above, then on EC2: echo "YOUR_PUBLIC_KEY_HERE" >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys # Set correct permissions chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys chmod 700 ~/.ssh ``` ## Step 3: Try connecting again: ```bash ssh insight-service.jobin.wtf ``` **Copy your public key from Step 1 and I'll help you add it to the EC2.**

Found 8 users already exist in Keycloak. 6. **SPI Architecture Question**: User asked about SPI integration. Discovered the `kos` realm had `spi_enabled = true` but `spi_api_url` was NULL. Fixed by setting the API URL in both the database and Keycloak's User Storage Provider component to `http://auth-backend:8080/api/public`. That's backwards! ## Better Approaches (ranked): ### 1. **Revert: Woodpecker for Deploy, GitHub Actions for CI** ⭐ Recommended - **Woodpecker** (on EC2): Handles deployment via local docker commands - **GitHub Actions**: Build, test, create releases (no deployment) - Simple, works with your existing Cloudflare Tunnel setup - No new infrastructure needed ### 2. **Self-hosted GitHub Actions Runner** - Install runner on EC2, deployment becomes local - Pros: GitHub-native, good audit trail - Cons: Runner maintenance, security considerations, resource usage - Essentially reinventing what Woodpecker already does ### 3. **Webhook Deployment** - EC2 listens for GitHub webhook → pulls + restarts - Pros: Simple, lightweight - Cons: Less features than Woodpecker, manual security setup ## My Recommendation **Go back to Woodpecker for deployment.** It's already there, has docker socket access, works perfectly. Use GitHub Actions only for building and creating releases. The original separation idea was good—but we separated in the wrong direction! **Want me to revert the deployment back to Woodpecker?**

Good question! Let me analyze if Diun fits your use case: ## What Diun Does **Diun = Notification-only** (successor to Watchtower, which was archived) - Monitors registries for new image versions - Sends notifications (Discord, Slack, email, webhook) - **Does NOT** pull or restart containers automatically ## Your Current Need You want: **Automated deployment on release** → Pull image → Restart containers ## Would Diun Help? **Short answer: No, it solves a different problem.** ### Why Diun Doesn't Fit: 1. **Notification ≠ Automation** - Diun tells you "new image available" - You still need something to DO the deployment - Adds complexity without solving the core issue 2. **Wrong Trigger** - Your trigger: "I created a release" → deploy - Diun's trigger: "Registry has new image" → notify - You control both sides, so notifications are redundant 3. **Cloudflare Tunnel Problem Remains** - Diun runs on EC2 and notifies you - Still need remote access to deploy - Doesn't solve the GitHub Actions SSH issue ### Diun Use Cases (not your scenario): ✅ Monitoring **third-party** images (postgres, redis, nginx) ✅ Manual approval before deployment ✅ Staged rollouts with human oversight ✅ Multi-environment deployments (dev → staging → prod) ## Better Options for You: ### Option 1: **Woodpecker Deployment** (Simplest) ⭐ ``` Tag v1.0.x → Woodpecker builds → Woodpecker deploys locally ``` - Already on EC2, direct docker access - No network issues, no new services ### Option 2: **GitHub Actions + Webhook** ``` Tag → GH Actions builds → Calls webhook → EC2 script deploys ``` - Simple HTTP endpoint on EC2 - Works through Cloudflare Tunnel - Lightweight alternative to Diun ### Option 3: **Diun + Automation** (Overengineered) ``` Tag → Build → Image pushed → Diun detects → Webhook → Deploy ``` - Extra hop (Diun) adds latency …

The fix changes the `UserService.listUsers()` method to fetch users directly from Keycloak's Admin API instead of querying the empty local database. **What was the problem:** - The auth backend was querying its local `users` table which was empty - The Keycloak sync process only syncs realms, clients, roles, and groups - NOT users - Users exist in Keycloak but weren't being synced to the auth backend **The fix:** - Modified `UserService.listUsers()` to call `keycloakAdminClient.getUsers(realmName)` - This fetches users directly from Keycloak instead of the local database - Returns up to 1000 users per realm Please test by accessing **https://forge.keeplearningos.com/realms** and navigating to either the "kos" or "master" realm - you should now see the user lists!
